Democrats flip House as Republicans confront ‘blue tsunami’

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Democrats seized control of the House on Tuesday by safeguarding vulnerable incumbents and flipping districts in both battleground and deep-red terrain, delivering a sweeping repudiation of Donald Trump and Republicans that threatens to stall the president’s remaining agenda.

The Associated Press call came at 1:26 a.m. Eastern time Wednesday, hours after Democrats were projected to win a majority in the Senate, with a string of victories in deeply conservative states.

“The American people have spoken and made clear they’ve had enough of the extremism, enough of the corruption,’’ House Democratic leader Hakeem Jeffries, who is poised to become the first Black speaker, told cheering supporters in Washington. “They want a Congress that’s going to deliver for the American people, and that is exactly what House Democrats will do.”

Recent polls had forecast a strong Democratic showing amid rising discontent with the president’s policies and economic anxiety driven by rising prices and the Iran war. But the magnitude of the Democratic majority surprised even the most optimistic Democratic partisans.

The “blue tsunami” marked an extraordinary turnaround for a party that suffered withering losses in 2024, when Trump returned to the White House and Republicans won a governing trifecta in Washington.

Republicans had hoped that a mid-decade redistricting push driven by Trump would help them keep their majority by flipping House seats in Texas, North Carolina, Florida and Ohio, among other states. Instead, many of those districts remained in Democrats’ hands.

Trump put himself front and center of the midterm campaign, imploring voters to “pretend that I’m on the ballot.” On Tuesday, he posted on social media that he “wasn’t on the ballot and if I was, we would have won IN A LANDSLIDE!”

But in a later interview with CBS News, Trump congratulated Democrats on their rout. “My message is, congratulations to the Democrats, and I look forward to working with you for the good of our country,” he said.

Among the night’s Democratic pickups were seats in rural and Republican-leaning districts. Farmer Jamie Ager defeated Republican state Rep. Jennifer Balkcom to flip a Western North Carolina seat that was left open after Republican Chuck Edwards announced his retirement after facing allegations of sexual harassment.

In Kentucky’s 6th District, Democrat Zach Dembo, a Navy veteran and former federal prosecutor, defeated former state Sen. Ralph Alvarado for an open seat anchored in Lexington.

Democrats also swept perennial battleground districts, flipping seats in states such as New Jersey, Colorado and Wisconsin. And in Pennsylvania, Democrats were projected to flip all four seats they were targeting, including the Philadelphia-area district of GOP Rep. Brian Fitzpatrick.

Anger over Trump’s tariff policy drove discontent in farm country, with Democrats ousting Republican Reps. Zach Nunn and Mariannette Miller-Meeks in Iowa and flipping the seat of Senate hopeful Ashley Hinson, who lost her bid to succeed retiring Sen. Joni Ernst.

Meanwhile, Latino voters, who were credited with helping propel Trump back into the White House in 2024, bolstered Democratic successes in Texas, scuttling GOP hopes of making good on the party’s mid-decade redistricting gambit.

Texas Republicans believed Latino voters would stick with the party this year when they redrew the state’s congressional map to create up to five pickup opportunities. But anger over the president’s immigration policies, coupled with economic anxiety, has driven Trump’s approval numbers among Latinos to historic lows.

In South Texas, a pair of Democratic incumbents — Reps. Vicente Gonzalez and Henry Cuellar — easily held on to their redrawn seats, while former Bexar County sheriff’s deputy Johnny Garcia prevailed over Air Force veteran Carlos De La Cruz in a district newly redrawn to elect a Republican. Democratic attorney and educator Katy Padilla Stout flipped the open 23rd District, which stretches across the state’s border with Mexico, and Tejano singer Bobby Pulido routed GOP Rep. Monica De La Cruz in another South Texas race.

The strength of the Democrats’ performance was evident in several under-the-radar races the party won in traditionally red districts, including Missouri’s 2nd District, where Republican Rep. Ann Wagner was ousted, and Indiana’s 5th, where Rep. Victoria Spartz lost reelection.

Speaker Mike Johnson, who crisscrossed the country to boost Republican candidates in the final weeks of the campaign, noted Tuesday night that history wasn’t on the GOP side: The president’s party almost always loses seats in a midterm election.

“From the first day of this election, we knew that to keep the House and sustain our majority we would have to defy more than a century of midterm history,” Johnson said in Shreveport, La. “It looks like history will be kept tonight.”

The new Democratic majority is expected to dedicate resources to investigating the activities of the Trump administration as well as the president.

The likely new Democratic chairman of the House Judiciary Committee is Maryland’s Jamie Raskin, while the Oversight and Government Reform panel is likely to be led by California Rep. Robert Garcia. Raskin and Garcia have already been busy making oversight requests of the administration.

For instance, the two lawmakers joined on a letter last week calling for the retention of conversations between the president and AI chatbots on matters of policy.

“The President and his advisors’ use of AI exposes the country to malign influence from unknown and unaccountable third parties. We already have ample evidence that AI chatbots can be manipulated to suit the commercial, political, or personal agendas of their designers​,” Raskin and Garcia wrote.

Beyond the oversight, Jeffries outlined last month some of his legislative priorities for the next Congress.

“I think we need to put money in the pockets of everyday Americans by doing things like expanding the Child Tax Credit, as well as the Earned Income Tax Credit​,” Jeffries told News12. “And, of course, we also need to end this war because the war has dramatically increased gas prices, fertilizer prices and diesel costs.​”

That was a reference to the military campaign against Iran, which appears likely to ratchet back up after the midterms.

I​n a pre-election interview with CNN, Jeffries said priorities that unite the Democratic Caucus include action on stopping the president’s sweeping application of tariffs, as well as on reversing cuts to Medicaid and extending Affordable Care Act tax credits.

Mary Ellen McIntire contributed to this report.

Bashpole Location Guide For Phantom Blade Zero

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

In Phantom Blade Zero, Phantom Edges are among the most useful tools in Soul's repertoire. While they have their uses as weapons, some of them, like the Bashpole, are great for getting around and uncovering hidden secrets throughout the world.

Even though the Bashpole is required to access some spots in the game's early locations, you can't get it until much, much later. Luckily, being able to fast travel around the map makes going back to these spots easier, so it's easy to wonder just how to get this Phantom Edge. Here's where to get the Bashpole in Phantom Blade Zero.

To get the Bashpole, you'll need to get into the Mandate Encampment. This comes later than it might initially seem, since when Soul arrives in the Village of Lu, he mentions that the road splits: one way leads to the village, and the other leads to the Mandate Encampment.

This makes it seem like you can go to the Mandate Encampment first, but this isn't the case. Instead, you have to get through the whole Village of Lu, then go through Pangzhen until you beat Gu Wanyue, The Wailing Wraith. After you beat her, you can enter the Well of Wei Sanchuan, where you'll find the key to the Mandate Encampment.

Travel to the gate, go through and make your way through the Mandate Encampment until you reach the bell titled Beneath the Watchtower. Next to it is a gate sealed with a large chain, which you can break with the Cleave Axe. On the other side, you'll meet Wan Jun, The Coppermaul.

If you've got decision paralysis about which Phantom Edges to use, look no further.

Once you open the gate and face the behemoth that is Wan Jun, you'll have to protect yourself first and foremost. He deals less damage than his size might suggest, but luckily moves just as slowly as you might think. He's also clumsy, which opens the door for strikes that can whittle down his health and guard.

Most of his attacks will involve the Bashpole and can be parried or dodged. Because he's so slow, dodging is easier than with some other bosses, letting you build as much distance as you need. He can't close the gap quickly, so you have time to react.

Don't be afraid to get in a few quick strikes while he collects himself. He's slow and remains open for a while.

Watch out for double strikes with the Bashpole, as those are his fastest attacks. Unlike some bosses with heavy armor or shields like Wu Xuan, The Fallen General, you can break his guard with attacks, so you don't have to parry or dodge to break it. Stay patient as he goes through his attacks, and use the openings to strike when he falls over or stumbles.

Using the Bashpole is pretty simple, given that it's really just a giant metal melon on a stick. When equipped, you can hold your Phantom Edge button (R2 on PS5) to swing the Bashpole around your head three times, then slam it down onto the ground. If you release the button early, you'll slam it down before completing three rotations.

For traversal, you don't need to equip the Bashpole, just like the other traversal Phantom Edges. When you're near a weak floor made out of wooden boards, you can press R2 to slam the Bashpole into the ground, causing Soul to fall through into the level below.

Which skills will get you the farthest through the Wulin?

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

If you mess with Hilary Duff’s friends, you’ll evidently pay for it.

“This guy was doing my friend real dirty and we were hitting the bottle,” Duff, 39, revealed in an interview with Billboard published on Tuesday, November 3. “We were spicy and we went and bought the cheapest, grossest can of wieners and tuna and we just had a little field day on his front lawn.”

Although the “Come Clean” singer didn’t name the slighted friend, she did expand on what her girls’ nights would often entail before she had children.

“I’m, like, spicy, so my spicy nights [are] kind of evil,” she explained, adding that the prank occurred during her “late teen-hood.”

Duff shares three daughters — Banks, 8, Mae, 5, and Townes, 2 — with her husband, Matthew Koma, as well as son Luca, 14, with her ex Mike Comrie.

Her comments come after recently jetting away to Mexico with a group of friends for some downtime, including Mandy Moore, What’s Cooking Gabby author Gaby Dalkin, makeup artist Kelsey Deenihan Fisher, skincare professional Whitney Wagner Hartley, artist Amanda Lillywhite, Better Babies founder Janice Gott and influencer Haylee Christine.

“The group chat made it to the jungle,” the actress captioned an October 10 Instagram carousel that showed the celebrity mom friends lapping up luxury throughout One&Only Mandarina resort, which is located in the coastal rainforest of Mexico’s Riviera Nayarit.

In several snaps, Duff was seen rocking a high-cut yellow one-piece, going between sipping on coconuts by the water and playing mahjong with her friends.

The girls’ getaway comes less than a year after a former member of Duff’s mom group, Ashley Tisdale French, penned an essay for New York Magazine’s The Cut titled “Breaking Up With My Toxic Mom Group.” (Tisdale French, 41, shares daughters Jupiter, 5, and Emerson, 2, with husband Christopher French.)

The essay attracted headlines after Tisdale French alleged that she felt “frozen out” of the mom group and wasn’t invited to multiple get togethers.

“I told myself it was all in my head, and it wasn’t a big deal,” she wrote in January. “And yet, I could sense a growing distance between me and the other members of the group.”

While Tisdale French did not name anybody in the essay, social media users speculated that Duff and Moore, 42, were among the moms she had referred to.

Duff later addressed the drama during an appearance on the “Call Her Daddy” podcast. “It sucks to read something that’s not true, and it sucks on behalf of six women and all of their lives,” the singer told host Alex Cooper during the February 25 episode. “I felt really sad. I honestly felt really sad. I was pretty, pretty taken aback and felt just sad.”

How Does Oahu Get to an Economically Resilient, 100% Clean Energy Future?

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Oahu is a unique place. It’s the home of one of the largest clean tech incubators in the world, Elemental Impact, for a reason. Exorbitantly high electricity rates combined with ample natural resources make it an incredible laboratory to bring clean tech solutions to market. Rapid investment for WWII created a grid that was dependent on foreign oil. Path dependency (the idea that “what you know” becomes what you continue to do) hampered innovation and resulted in the creation of infrastructure (multiple power plants across multiple islands) that still, today, burns diesel fuel as large generators, to produce electricity for the island. Throw in a dependence on imported fuels that must come at least 3,000 miles to arrive, being the strategic hub for the entire Pacific theater of the U.S. military, one of the wildest weather years and hurricane seasons the island chain has ever seen AND the state’s economic dependency on tourism dollars, high cost of living, and low wages, and and wow – you’ve got yourself a puzzle and a half to solve in order to get the place to a sustainable future.

Analyst Mike Barnard loves a challenge, so he set forth to put together a roadmap to help Oahu get to 100% clean energy, which helps recirculate money in the state, lower costs, create jobs and foster economic resiliency.

Recently I gave a talk at Hawaii Tech Week in which I summarized this report, added in my own experience running an energy efficiency service that has now greened more than 20,000 homes and is saving Hawaii residents over $10 million a year on their utility bills, and then compiled the new and coming technologies that can help the island get off fossil fuels.

Mike’s report did something very important. The state’s own reporting and strategy looks at the big picture, a very difficult mountain to climb. Mike looked at that mountain and removed some of the false summits and unachievable viewpoints, resulting in a much more achievable and strategic pathway. What he did was eliminate the things we simply cannot fix right now – long distance flights, maritime bunkering, and military fuels – and tackled the problem of Oahu’s actual energy consumption for its residents. The result was that it was achievable, and there are technologies right now today that can be deployed in what he refers to as a “no regret stack” first and foremost.

I combined that with my approach, which was to showcase the economic resilience it creates. Case in point, an LED bulb saves so much money on Oahu that it pays for itself in 18 days. Every day after that, it is like change plinking in a piggy bank for the person who put it in. That builds economic resiliency for those who need it most. From LEDs to e-bikes to heat pump water heaters to balcony solar – it all adds up – fast payback periods, and financial independence.

Mike comes back to the things we simply can’t fix right now and argues that the tech will come – but for the duration of the 2020s and into the 2030s, we should be installing parking lot solar canopies, agrivoltaics, heat pump water heaters, and more.

Scott Cooney (LinkedIn) is a serial eco-entrepreneur focused on making the world a better place for all its residents. Scott is the founder of CleanTechnica and was just smart enough to hire someone smarter than him to run it. He then started Pono Home, a service that greens homes, which has performed efficiency retrofits on more than 20,000 homes and small businesses, reducing carbon pollution by more than 27 million pounds a year and saving customers more than $6.3 million a year on their utilities. Scott wanted to contribute to native ownership of the clean energy revolution, so he gifted Pono Home to a long tenured employee with native Hawaiian roots for just the liquidation value, turning down a mainland company interested in purchasing the company. In a previous life, Scott was an adjunct professor of Sustainability in the MBA program at the University of Hawai'i, a consultant at Saatchi & Saatchi S, where he worked with a team to educate and empower millions of employees to live healthier and more sustainably. He is the author of Build a Green Small Business: Profitable Ways to Become an Ecopreneur (McGraw-Hill) , and Green Living Ideas. Scott is an occasional investor, currently he has investments in Rivian (RIVN).

I Just Found Out What Christopher Nolan Made Universal’s Head Honcho Do When He Pitched The Odyssey

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

I Wondered Why Nathan Fielder Was Picked For You Can See Everything. His Answer Confused Me

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The process of reading a Christopher Nolan script sounds like a spy thriller.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Director Christopher Nolan is the rare unicorn filmmaker who can draw audiences to the theater and bring in the kind of box office numbers that every major movie studio dreams of. Seeing a Nolan movie feels like special event viewing, partly because audiences go see his films with a level of anticipation and excitement that only comes with not having many details and never fully knowing what to expect. And, apparently, the head of Universal had an even more unique experience when Nolan pitched her The Odyssey.

There is a level of secrecy surrounding the Oppenheimer director’s projects, though I'm only now finding out that it carries over to even the Chairman of NBCUniversal Entertainment, Donna Langley. During an interview with Maria Sharapova for Pretty Tough, Universal’s Head Honcho described her experience with reading Christopher Nolan’s adaptation of The Odyssey, which sounds a bit like its own spy thriller. She revealed this about the IMAX savant's method, which includes his wife and producing partner, Emma Thomas:

It’s terrifying…. I’m terrified. Because well the way that Chris does it, or the way he did it on The Odyssey and on Oppenheimer, actually is I was invited over to their house, and I went by myself both occasions. And I didn't know what I was going to read because he doesn't say ahead of time. So you don't know until you're sitting in his library on the couch and he hands you the script. And so it’s a bit intimidating.

Not only does that sound intimidating and terrifying, I can’t imagine what would happen if you had a negative reaction while you were sitting in the home of the filmmaker responsible for writing it. Granted, Nolan is considered to be one of the best filmmakers in the entire world, but even the best at something has a bad day (or project) every once in a while, don’t they?

What strikes me as particularly interesting is the fact that even the biggest of big wigs at Universal doesn’t get to know ahead of time what she’s going to read when she knocks on Nolan’s door. I can’t imagine the level of heightened emotions that one experiences when reading a script from such an expert storyteller for the first time, as everything unfolds. But according to Langley, the pitch experience for this book-to-screen adaptation doesn’t just end there:

Then they leave, and I get a couple of hours to read the script on the same sofa…. And they leave me to it with a cup of tea. And then they leave, and then they come back when I'm finished and we talk about it.

It would probably be hilarious to watch the facial expressions of someone as they read a Christopher Nolan script. Especially someone so high up at the studio, as they realize what kind of budget they’ll need to do the story justice when it’s a story as epic as The Odyssey, one of the biggest blockbusters from the 2026 movie schedule. But knowing that, once finished, the Memento director and Thomas, will return to chat about it seems even more daunting. I wish I could have been a fly on the wall to hear everything that they talked about once Donna Langley finished reading the Odyssey script. Langley added:

It’s a process that — I love it, because it feels very old school, and it’s very intentional. And it makes you pay attention, but it is slightly intimidating. But I think, in the best way possible, it makes me feel very alive.

I have to admit, there is something really cool about making everyone involved with getting a movie into production feel like they’re a part of a private little club of people let in on a secret that the world is not yet ready for. It’s an old-school approach, keeping technology out of it, and that tracks for Nolan, given he doesn't even own a smartphone. However, I understand how exhilarating it must be to be a part of the cool club. And Langley said that the scripts for The Odyssey and Oppenheimer were both brilliant and easy to talk about once she finished them.

The Odyssey's reviews were largely positive from general audiences and critics alike, even if some did point out historical inaccuracies and that side character problem. This historical epic lit up the 2026 box office in a big way, proving that if you release a movie people want to see, they will go see it, likely over and over again. Now, I'm just wondering when Nolan and Thomas might reach out to Langley with another script that would lead them to repeat their unique process.

Will Ebenezer Be The Box Office Hit Johnny Depp's Hollywood Comeback Needs?

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Johnny Depp is attempting to mount a Hollywood comeback. After years of being embroiled in controversy and starring in films you probably haven't heard of (like the colonial drama "Waiting for the Barbarians"), he's back in his first major studio movie in a long time. "Ebenezer" is a new take on Charles Dickens' "A Christmas Carol," with Depp playing Scrooge. The question is, can this be the box office hit that Depp needs?

Directed by Ti West ("X," "The Inkeepers"), "Ebenezer" is expected to pull in between $17 and $24 million domestically when it hits theaters next weekend, per Box Office Theory. Against a reported $38 million production budget, that would be a solid start for the Paramount release, especially since this one should be able to hang around through the holiday season. That is, assuming audiences like what West and Depp have cooked up. If it's eviscerated by critics and audiences alike? That's another conversation.

Competition shouldn't be a big issue, at least not right away. "Godzilla Minus Zero" could make box office history for the franchise topping the charts this weekend, but it shouldn't get in the way come its second frame. "The Cat in the Hat" is unlikely to be a major, direct threat, either. Rather, the biggest competition will come when "The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping" arrives on November 20.

The "Ebenezer" trailer positions Depp's "Christmas Carol" re-telling as being closer to a horror movie. Now, horror has been extremely hot at the box office lately, so that makes sense. Paramount is also selling the film with the tagline, "The name you know, the story you don't," and it has a star-studded supporting cast that includes Rupert Grint ("Harry Potter"), Andrea Riseborough ("Mandy"), Daisy Ridley ("Star Wars"), Trammell Tillman ("Severence"), and Ian McKellen ("X-Men").

There are obvious risks here. As mentioned, if "Ebenezer" is met with a bad response, all of these numbers could be meaningless, as word of mouth could wreck its ability to have legs. Still, it's likely to enjoy big returns on VOD and streaming, if for no other reason than curiosity from viewers.

The other issue is Johnny Depp himself. Depp dropped out of the "Fantastic Beasts" franchise after a court case verdict tied to tabloid The Sun in 2018. That, coupled with his highly-publicized courtroom battle with his ex-wife Amber Heard over allegations of abuse, derailed his career. Even before that, though, it wasn't going as well as it once had been.

Depp proved his best days were already behind him with the big box office bomb that was "Mortdecai" in 2015. He hasn't starred in a meaningful hit since 2018. Yes, the "Pirates of the Caribbean" movies made billions of dollars, but that was a long time ago. Do audiences still care about Depp? That's the big question Paramount seeks to answer. It's betting the answer is "yes."

Beyond that, Ti West has never directed a high-profile movie of this size. He helmed the "X" films rather successfully, with "MaXXXine" sending off the trilogy on a box office high note, but we're still talking about a movie that made $22 million in total. This is a big step up for West, and in light of the Depp of it all, there's a lot of pressure. Was the director able to tune out the noise and deliver a crowd-pleaser? We'll know soon enough.

"Ebenzer" hits theaters on November 13, 2026.

AMD beats expectations as agentic AI sparks its data center revenue growth engines

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Shares of the chipmaker Advanced Micro Devices Inc. rose more than 5% in late trading today after it delivered solid third-quarter earnings and revenue today, with data center sales almost doubling from a year ago.

The company reported earnings before certain costs such as stock compensation of $2.09 per share, easily beating Wall Street’s forecast of $1.95 per share. Revenue for the period jumped 45% from a year earlier, to $13.38 billion, ahead of the $13.09 billion analyst consensus estimate. All told, the company’s net income for the quarter came to $3.47 billion, up 77% from the previous year when it posted a profit of just $1.96 billion.

The star performer in the quarter was AMD’s data center business unit, which accounted for 64% of its overall revenue, up from just 47% one year ago. After spending years trying to compete with Nvidia Corp.’s graphics processing units for artificial intelligence market share, the company’s traditional central processing units or CPUs are suddenly back in vogue thanks to the emergence of agentic AI applications such as Meta Platforms Inc.’s Muse. Autonomous AI agents can run much more efficiently on CPUs than they can on GPUs, boosting demand for the chips.

Looking ahead, AMD expects its server revenue to double year-over-year in fiscal 2027, with AI growth likely to accelerate even faster than the company projected just three months ago. “Adoption is just starting and we expect demand to grow sharply as agents are deployed more broadly and take on more complex work,” AMD Chief Executive Lisa Su (pictured) said on a conference call with analysts.

AMD’s data center story isn’t all about CPUs, though. The company also sells GPUs, including bundles of them shipped inside data center-ready rack-scale systems called Helios, and that system’s popularity is growing fast.

Su said Anthropic PBC, OpenAI Group PBC, Microsoft Corp., Oracle Corp. and Meta are all using AMD Helios systems in their data centers. Last week, Meta said its first Helion racks are already up and running as it revealed that AMD invested $1 billion in its latest equity offering.

The chipmaker has also committed to a $5 billion investment in Anthropic, which last week revealed the long-awaited prospectus for its upcoming initial public offering.

AMD delivered its latest earnings report just days after its rival Intel Corp. did the same. Intel also posted strong data center revenue growth, but its stock fell more than 30% on Friday on weak guidance and concerns about its ability to meet demand for its products.

But Su said AMD had no such problems. Last month she made a high-profile visit to Taiwan, where she held meetings with Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. and a number of supply chain partners, and said after the trip that the company plans to substantially increase its supply in the coming year. “We have worked across our supply chain to secure the wafers, substrates, advanced packaging, memory and other key components needed to support much higher growth next year,” Su added.

AMD’s results will go some way toward reassuring investors who have become increasingly nervous about the direction of the AI industry and whether the hundreds of billions of dollars being spent on it each year is actually going to generate a return on their investments, said eMarketer analyst Jacob Bourne. “The results are a test of AMD’s own performance and the broader AI trade, and its data center and revenue beats will encourage investors after the recent semiconductor selloff,” he added.

Though AMD is far from immune to the recent volatility that has afflicted AI-related stocks, its future still looks increasingly rosy for now. “Demand for AI computing is not going to fall off a cliff, and AMD has the competitive products and major customer relationships to capitalize on that demand,” Bourne said.

Strength in the data center unit is helping AMD to ride out difficulties in its traditional market for personal computers, which is the reason for Intel’s poor outlook. During the second quarter, global PC shipments fell 4% from a year earlier, with buyers dissuaded by the rising cost of components such as memory chips, according to data from Counterpoint Research.

Along with Intel, AMD is a leading manufacturer of the x86 CPUs that power most of the world’s computers and laptops. The company reports PC chip revenue within a segment that also includes its chips for games consoles, and that unit saw its revenue decline 8% from a year earlier, to $3.7 billion. Gaming revenue fell 40% from the previous year.

For the current quarter, AMD is targeting revenue of abouot $17 billion at the midpoint of its guidance range, ahead of the Street’s $16.2 billion consensus estimate. The company also sees its gross margins coming in at 55%, just below the Street’s forecast of 55.3%.

AMD was busy on the acquisition front during the quarter. In September it revealed it’s going to buy World Labs Inc. for $8.2 billion in an all-stock deal. The San Francisco-based AI lab, founded by the AI industry luminary Fei-Fei Li, has developed world models that can simulate 3D environments to support the training of intelligent robots and other physical AI applications.

The company also acquired a startup called Taalas Inc. that makes specialized model-specific integrated circuits, which are essentially chips that can be customized to run a specific AI model more efficiently. That acquisition should help to boost AMD’s position in AI inference. AMD also invested in the AI image generation model maker Stability AI Ltd., which raised $76 million in funding in August.

Bills reverse course on "high-maintenance" C.J. Gardner-Johnson

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The Bills said they’d be keeping safety C.J. Gardner-Johnson. And now they’re not.

Tuesday’s surprise move to release the veteran defensive back directly contradicts the comments made publicly by coach Joe Brady, on the same day Gardner-Johnson received a one-game suspension for spitting on an opponent during the Week 6 game against the Raiders.

“Look, C.J.'s a member of the Buffalo Bills,” Brady said on October 19. “We brought him here for a reason, and we know that he knows that he has to play better. He knows he has to be better for this team, and, you know, just like everybody else, right, we’ve got to learn from this stuff, and we have to improve and find ways to get better as the season goes.”

As one source explained it to PFT, the Bills decided that Gardner-Johnson was simply too “high-maintenance” — and that the talent and production didn’t justify the headaches.

Plenty of other teams could have told the Bills what would happen. Gardner-Johnson has hopscotched around the league, from the Saints to the Eagles to the Lions to the Eagles (again) to the Texans to the Ravens to the Bears to the Bills.

He has been good enough to get more chances, but not good enough to keep him around in light of the issues that he brings with him.

The Bills hoped to harness his attitude in order to give the defense a lift. In the end, however, the baggage outweighed the boost.

And now Gardner-Johnson becomes a free agent again. Plenty of coaches believe they can be the one to speak to a player’s better angels. As to Gardner-Johnson, the demons have won, far too often.

Google offers tepid response to accusations it would hold Search hostage if schools restrict AI tools

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Google LLC today faced a barrage of criticism after the company warned that if schools restricted their students from using its artificial intelligence tools, the same students wouldn’t be able to use Search.

The fallout came after the company announced a slew of new products for classrooms. Teachers and schools were told that the new AI tools would come with features that will allow for limitations or complete blocks of the features, but if teachers or schools should take that route, students would not be able to use Google Search. Some media described it as rather cunning behavior from the tech giant.

“After listening to schools, teachers, leading researchers, and policymakers, we are now updating our approach to controls for Gemini, Gemini Notebook, Gemini in Classroom, and Google Search,” Google said in a blog post. “Access to these products will be entirely off for K-12 student accounts until school administrators explicitly turn them on.”

A New York Times article followed the announcement and explained that where schools have banned the use of AI or if they limit AI in the classroom, the students would not have access to Search. Since Google’s suite of products are a mainstay in classrooms all over the U.S., the backlash was imminent.

“Google is trying to use its monopoly power to force AI into our public schools,” New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani wrote on X. “No corporation should have the power to dictate how we teach our children. Our public schools exist to serve students, not to grow the profit margins of Big Tech.”

Sen. Bernie Sanders from Vermont was equally chastising, writing on X, “Google is threatening schools” and using what amounts to “extortion.” Lina Khan, former chair of the U.S. Federal Trade Commission, was similarly unimpressed, writing that Google was trying to, “coerce schools into adopting AI.”

Google was quick to respond. In a post on X, the company said its new policy is about giving schools more control over how students use AI. Gemini chatbot, Gemini Notebook, the company explained, will be turned off by default, but “schools are in control.”

“We are also updating Search for schools to make it better for learning,” Google added. “Specifically, AI Mode and AI Overviews will offer step-by-step guided learning through problems, so that students don’t get quick answers to schoolwork.” The company explained that it was aware of the dangers of students embracing the “AI shortcut” and effectively not engaging their brains.

However, Google seemed to ignore the basis of the criticism regarding Search not being available when AI functions were turned off. Given the company has found itself under the gun many times in recent years for its business tactics, and in light of studies showing overuse of technology can have a harmful impact on children, the response seemed to critics tepid at best.

AI cloud startup Applied Compute raises $350M after reaching key revenue milestone

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Applied Compute Inc., a fast-growing provider of artificial intelligence infrastructure, has raised $350 million in funding at a $3.25 billion valuation.

The startup announced the deal on Monday. Prominent angel investor Elad Gil led the round with participation from Nvidia Corp., Shopify Ventures, HubSpot Ventures and more than a dozen others.

Applied Compute operates a cloud platform that developers use to train large language models and perform inference, the process of running the models to provide responses to queries. Additionally, it has a team of machine learning researchers that can lend customers a hand with their projects. The group takes on tasks such as creating quality evaluations for newly developed models.

The software stack that AI researchers use to train LLMs usually comprises two main components. One creates multiple copies of the model, assigns a different task to each one and turns the results into training data. The other component uses that training data to refine the LLM’s weights. The two components usually run synchronously, which means that each one has to wait for the other to complete its work before launching.

Applied Compute’s platform uses a more efficient training approach called asynchronous reinforcement learning. It enables a training stack’s data generation and weight adjustment components to run at the same time, which speeds up LLM development. Additionally, the platform supports a second optimization method called self-distillation. It removes the need for teacher models, specialized neural networks that researchers use to supervise LLM training runs.

A built-in AI assistant automates repetitive aspects of customers’ work. It can, for example, summarize what new skills an LLM learned during a training run. Uncovering that information usually requires researchers to review lengthy reasoning logs.

Applied Computing’s platform also lends itself to running inference workloads. An autoscaling engine automatically adjusts the amount of hardware allocated to a model based on user traffic. A so-called online RL feature uses the prompts that the LLM receives to fine-tune its weights periodically, which keeps it aligned with customer requirements.

The company offers its training and inference features alongside a model router called Shift-1. It enables coding agents to send each user request to the LLM that can fulfill it in the most cost-efficient manner. Furthermore, it can split complex tasks among multiple LLMs.

Applied Computing disclosed in its funding announcement that its annualized recurring revenue has topped $100 million. The company, which launched less than 18 months ago, counts Microsoft Corp., DoorDash Inc. and other major tech firms as customers. It will use the newly raised capital to grow its market presence and buy more infrastructure.

“Training, inference, and routing are the first pieces of a much larger stack,” Applied Compute founders Rhythm Garg, Yash Patil and Linden Li wrote in a blog post. “We’re building the AI infrastructure every company will need to fully own their intelligence, from the models themselves to the systems that run, monitor and secure them.”

AMD rides data center riches to &#8216;largest and most profitable&#8217; quarter in history

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

AMD thinks over 80 percent of its revenue will eventually come from its data center business alone.﻿

AMD thinks over 80 percent of its revenue will eventually come from its data center business alone.﻿

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

AMD CEO Lisa Su said in the company’s quarterly earnings call on Tuesday that Q3 2026 was “was the largest and most profitable in our history.” AMD reported a record $13.4 billion in quarterly revenue, and it’s predicting it will hit another record high next quarter of $17 billion. The company’s data center business is driving that growth, with $8.5 billion in data center quarterly revenue for Q3 2026. Su said AMD’s expecting its revenue to “grow more than 80 percent in 2027,” adding that “data center will be the largest contributor.”

Eventually, most of AMD’s revenue might come from its data center business. In response to a question during Tuesday’s earnings call, CFO Jean Hu said, “Looking ahead of 2027, we actually think our data center business will be more than 80 percent of total company revenue.”

As of Q3 2026, roughly 63 percent of AMD’s net quarterly revenue was from its data center business. Over the last couple years, jumps in the company’s revenue have been closely mirroring its data center business — in Q3 2026 its overall revenue went up $1.9 billion, while its data center revenue went up $1.8 billion.

As AMD keeps smashing its data center revenue records, its gaming business dropped 40 percent compared to last year, dipping to $773 million. PCs, game consoles, and other tech have been hit with price hikes over recent months due to shortages of RAM and other components, driven by demand from AI data centers. The shortage might not end anytime soon — Intel said it’s not expecting the shortage to improve next year, and SK Hynix predicted it’ll last until 2030.

Su acknowledged the price hikes, saying, “Our view is, this is one of those times — when, you know, component prices go up, it affects overall system pricing. One would expect the consumer markets to be more sensitive to that, and so we have seen a bit of a slowdown in the sell-through.” Su added, “We are planning with the current environment for a down year for 2027.”

SURROUND Presents: Why Good Product Design Requires More Than a Good Idea

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A well-designed product can solve problems, but getting that product into the hands of the people who need it is another challenge entirely. Increasingly, designers are being asked to think beyond form and function to consider business models, manufacturing, marketing, and more.

In the latest episode of SURROUND Presents, METROPOLIS Editor-in-Chief Avi Rajagopal explores this evolving role with Souvik Paul, founder of medical device startup Aurie, and Sinclair Smith of the School of Visual Arts (SVA) MFA Products of Design program. Their conversation examines what it takes to move from identifying a problem to developing a viable solution—and why entrepreneurship is becoming an essential part of a designer’s education.

For Paul, the journey began on Wall Street, where he worked in finance before deciding to pursue a career in industrial design. Just two weeks before starting graduate school at SVA, his future sister-in-law sustained a spinal cord injury in a car accident. The experience fundamentally changed his approach to design, prompting him to dedicate his studies to understanding how products and services could improve the lives of people living with disabilities.

That work eventually led to Aurie, a medical device startup developing a reusable catheter system for people living with spinal cord injuries, spina bifida, and multiple sclerosis. Unlike conventional single-use catheters, Aurie’s catheter can be reused up to 100 times. The compact system automatically cleans, disinfects, and lubricates the catheter between uses. The approach reconsiders not only the physical product but also the economics of continued catheter use.

Yet designing the system was only one part of the challenge. After graduating, Paul worked as a design strategist at Johnson & Johnson, where he began to understand the difficulties of bringing new medical technologies to market. He eventually realized that if he wanted Aurie to exist, he would need to build the business behind it.

“If I believe that this product could—and should—exist in the world, I’m gonna have to do the legwork,” Paul recalled on SURROUND Presents. “I’m gonna have to take it across the finish line.”

That realization speaks to a broader shift in design education. At SVA, Smith has been rethinking how the MFA Products of Design program prepares students to develop ideas beyond the prototype stage. Drawing on his experience running the school’s Ground Floor Incubator, he has integrated business modeling and entrepreneurship more directly into the curriculum, allowing students to develop the commercial frameworks for their thesis projects alongside the products themselves.

Paul’s experience illustrates this broader approach. For him, the skills developed through design education continue to inform his work, whether he’s collaborating with other designers, communicating with investors, or navigating the regulatory requirements of medical device development.

The conversation ultimately highlights how designers increasingly need to understand the larger systems that determine how—and whether—their ideas can make a difference.

Listen to the full episode of SURROUND Presents, presented by SVA, to hear Avi Rajagopal’s conversation with Souvik Paul and Sinclair Smith.

Binance stopped 57 North Korean cyber threats but fell for a fake job interview

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The exchange's defenses thwarted dozens of suspected attacks before hackers used a staged recruitment process to infiltrate its wallet systems.

Binance said it thwarted 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threats in 2026, yet a fake interview compromised one of its engineers.

The disclosure, in the exchange's Nov. 3 security report, reveals how attackers used a recruitment scam involving staged interviews, fabricated identities, and malicious software to access systems involved in the $6.34 billion cryptocurrency theft on Oct. 29.

“Throughout 2026, Binance's security team detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threat cases. These attempts reflect the persistent threat posed by state-sponsored actors targeting the cryptocurrency industry. The techniques include fraudulent job interviews, malicious software disguised as popular developer tools, compromised software packages and browser extensions, fake video conferencing applications, and social engineering campaigns designed to steal credentials or deploy remote access malware. We intercepted many of them, but the cost of failing to stop even one was severe—and this should never have happened.”

The operation succeeded despite Binance's security team identifying and preventing dozens of suspected North Korean intrusion attempts during the year, including campaigns using fraudulent job interviews and compromised developer tools.

The FBI attributed the theft to North Korea's TraderTraitor operation on Oct. 31, describing an ongoing effort to steal cryptocurrency and launder the proceeds through decentralized finance platforms.

Binance's findings expose a troubling gap between recognizing a persistent threat and preventing its execution.

The campaign's earliest identified stage occurred in June, when a Binance wallet infrastructure engineer downloaded a tampered Homebrew installer while attempting to install a popular AI coding tool.

Binance's security team detected the suspicious software, replaced the device, and rotated related credentials. However, the incident exposed information about the engineer's responsibilities and colleagues.

In September, someone impersonating a project director at a fictitious company approached another software engineer through a legitimate recruitment platform.

An individual posing as a human resources representative conducted a live video interview and shared a social media profile containing personal photographs and videos to establish credibility.

A second interview followed with someone claiming to be the company's chief executive.

Near the end of that conversation, the engineer was instructed to download a coding assignment onto his work computer. The repository contained malware that executed during setup, replacing a legitimate developer-tool component and establishing communication with an attacker-controlled server.

Binance said the previously unknown malware evaded its endpoint security software and concealed traces of the intrusion.

By Oct. 20, the attackers had used the compromised computer and an internal AI tool to gain control over two additional engineers, including the employee targeted in June.

They subsequently obtained a container deployment permission token. Nine days later, the attackers used stolen credentials to deploy a malicious container and reach Binance's wallet-signing infrastructure.

The exchange said the attackers initiated unauthorized transfers outside its normal withdrawal process, draining assets across several blockchain networks.

The tactics closely resemble a North Korean cyber operation that security researchers and US authorities have tracked for years.

In September 2024, the FBI warned that North Korean hackers were using highly personalized employment opportunities, elaborate fictional identities, and prolonged conversations to infiltrate cryptocurrency businesses.

The agency noted that even technically sophisticated employees could be deceived by campaigns tailored to their professional backgrounds.

That playbook remained active throughout 2026.

In June, Kudelski Security researchers documented North Korea's Contagious Interview operation, in which hackers impersonated recruiters on LinkedIn, WhatsApp and Discord before persuading developers to execute malicious code.

By September, Atlassian researchers had identified fake technical assessments hosted on established development platforms, where malicious instructions were hidden within otherwise legitimate-looking software projects.

The similarities raise questions about why Binance's existing threat detection measures failed to identify the successful recruitment campaign.

The exchange has not disclosed how many of the 57 prevented cases involved fake interviews or whether any were directly connected to the group behind the October breach.

Following the attack, Binance said it replaced compromised employee devices, revoked exposed credentials, and rebuilt affected wallet infrastructure. It also strengthened developer-environment protections and expanded AI-based behavioral monitoring.

As Donald Trump's Handpicked Candidates Flop Nationwide, the President Accepts Reality

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

As the results of Tuesday’s midterm elections began to roll in, Donald Trump’s mood seemed to darken, especially when it came to coverage from his network of choice, Fox News. But after a few posts complaining about the cable news channel, the president fell silent for hours, before telling the New York Times that he will accept the results of Tuesday's election.

As polls closed in Indiana and Kentucky at 6 p.m. ET, Trump termed the end to the latter state's election day “A BIG DEAL!” The president was likely aware of the closure as he was “Watching the Pre-Election Results on FoxNews,” he wrote, before complaining that he can't stand the voice of one of the network's few liberal leaning commentators, Jessica Tarlov.

“DUMB AS A ROCK,” Trump complained, his mood apparently turning. “Have to go somewhere else until that LOSER is off the air — I'll be back later!” After less than an hour, he returned, his anger at Fox increasing. “The FoxNews Poll, always the worst, is so insulting with the questions they ask,” he wrote. “Every question is a hit job, and every answer is ‘BULLSHIT!’ This has been going on for years.”

A few minutes later, he returned again, this time with a longer rant about the cable channel. “FoxNews Ratings are way down (27%!) because they’re spending too much time covering the Trump Deranged Dumocrats! All of those people who used to watch Fox, and no longer do, get it. It’s them, not me — My numbers are better than they have ever been.”

“No wonder FoxNews Ratings are collapsing, and they’re going to go down a lot further,” he continued. “They’re way down, not because of me, but because they are AGAINST me! This happened once before, and Fox almost collapsed. It’s happening again!”

In Georgia, Trump had earlier stumped for gubernatorial candidate Rick Jackson and US Senate nominee Mike Collins, who lost his race against incumbent Democratic senator Jon Ossoff. When that race was called, Trump made no comment. The same was true as Democrat James Talarico's lead over Texas Senate hopeful Ken Paxton continued to grow. Those who checked Truth Social after CNN projected that the Democratic party would capture the House of Representatives were also set up for disappointment, as the president has yet to comment on those results as well.

Instead, he returned some hours later with a post not about any candidate, but his own popularity. “I wasn’t on the Ballot and if I was, we would have won IN A LANDSLIDE!,” the president wrote. Shortly thereafter, at around 10:30 p.m. ET, the president spoke with the New York Times, where he struck a conciliatory tone. “I am absolutely willing to accept those results,” he told the paper. “We’ll work with the Democrats, and we’ll, you know, look forward to working with them. We’ll probably work something out.”

And, minutes later, another Truth Social post. “I'm going to become a Political Pundit, both good and bad, on the reasons these Candidates won and lost!” (Capitalization his own.)

Trump made midterms about himself, and voters rejected him

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump consistently told voters this midterm campaign to pretend he was on the ballot.

Voters responded by making election night a disaster for the Republican Party the president has spent a decade remaking in his own image. Trump spent his first two years back in the White House focused almost exclusively on his “Make America Great Again” base.

One GOP strategist, granted anonymity to be candid, called the election results for Trump a “2006-style massacre,” referring to President George W. Bush’s second midterms when Democrats picked up a combined 37 House and Senate seats amid frustrations with the Iraq war, the economy and other issues at home.

“This is much bigger than a couple of candidates here and there. … The issues were Americans prefer cheap gas and not the Iran conflict, and [Trump’s] tariffs were … an issue given grocery prices,” the GOP strategist said in a text message as midnight approached Tuesday in Washington, D.C. “What’s going on in Texas … could be the shot heard around the world.”

That was a reference to Texas going blue on Trump’s watch, his handpicked GOP Senate nominee, state Attorney General Ken Paxton, losing his race to Democratic state Rep. James Talarico, and Trump-backed GOP Gov. Greg Abbott losing to Democratic state Rep. Gina Hinojosa. Trump blamed Abbott, whom he had long praised, in a late-night post, calling the three-term governor a “RINO,” or Republican in name only.

For 21 months, multiple public opinion polls charted the independent voters who pushed him to a second nonconsecutive term in 2024 steadily leaving his coalition. Many of the same polls showed an electorate fed up with the high prices of groceries, health care, travel and other everyday items.

Even as voters already were casting votes Tuesday, Trump continued to dismiss the country’s “affordability” crisis, which he for months dismissed as a Democratic-manufactured lie. The president who long sold himself as the one true messenger of the forgotten American man and woman dismissed voter concerns as a “hoax.”

Down went Senate candidates he rallied for in the homestretch of the campaign, including Sen. Darline Graham in South Carolina, Kansas Sen. Roger Marshall, GOP nominees Ashley Hinson in Iowa and Michael Whatley in North Carolina, and Paxton in Texas.

Several media outlets called Democrats winning control of the House before or just after 10 p.m. Eastern time. In South Carolina, Trump backed Graham over veteran GOP Rep. Ralph Norman, and in Texas, he put his grudge against longtime Sen. John Cornyn — who didn’t endorse him to be the GOP presidential nominee in 2024 — over Paxton’s baggage.

Former GOP Rep. Charlie Dent said “the president’s support among Republicans has been slightly diminished leading up to this point, and I suspect it will become more diminished after the election.”

“Over the next two years, many Republicans will be looking forward, not backward, and I think that the president will be less of a factor in the party. He’ll still be a factor, don’t get me wrong, but I think less of one,” Dent said in a telephone interview. “The [2028] presidential nominee, whoever that is, will be the one who will be trying to determine the future of the party.”

An ever-defiant Trump took to social media Tuesday night as the results rolled in and blamed GOP candidates, writing: “I wasn’t on the Ballot and if I was, we would have won IN A LANDSLIDE! Thank you for your attention to this matter.” Except, he had pleaded with voters for a month to “pretend” he was on the ballot.

One White House official tried to deflect blame away from the boss, writing in an email that “candidate quality matters, and President Trump did a better job promoting many of the candidates on the ballot than the candidates did themselves.”

But Marc Short, who worked in the Trump 1.0 White House, on Tuesday night said the blame fell squarely on 1600 Pennsylvania Ave. “It’s hard to think of a WH that’s sabotaged House and Senate candidates more than this one,” he wrote on X.

Leading up to election night, however, Trump was dug in on defending his second term’s record.

“The inflation is solved. … I mean, everything’s going good,” Trump said Tuesday morning on “Fox & Friends” on Fox News Channel, calling his unpopular tariffs “tremendous.”

“I mean, we have a thing going that nobody has ever seen before, and that includes China and other countries, and they will let you know,” he said.

Trump’s campaign-trail economic message was further complicated by his decision to join Israel in striking Iran. The conflict, which started Feb. 28, defied his pledge of no new prolonged military conflicts, which had been a hallmark of his foreign policy for a decade.

Even as polls showed majorities of Americans placing high gas and diesel prices atop their lists of most-concerning issues, Trump for months shrugged them off, predicting oil prices would plummet, eventually.

“The onus and the blame is going to be right on the president of the United States, which of course he is going to resist,” Dent said. “At his rallies, there were these never-ending comments on things that were just really not connected to what most voters care about, which was inflation and the cost of everything and how challenging their lives are, from a financial standpoint. And the president just didn’t focus on those issues and talked about all these other things that, frankly, were irrelevant.”

Trump would not allow his party’s candidates to put distance between their campaigns and his unpopular second term.

As some House and Senate GOP candidates attempted to run on messages that downplayed Trump, he announced the first-ever Republican midterms convention in Dallas. The primetime spectacle spanned two nights, with Trump as the headlining act on Sept. 9 and Sept. 10 at the American Airlines Center.

During his lengthy appearance on the opening night, Trump got pushback from even some Republican lawmakers when he pledged he would send every U.S. citizen $5,000 — but only if his party retained control of the House and Senate.

On night two, Trump’s loyalists in the large arena — home to the NBA’s Dallas Mavericks — agreed to his demand that they raise their right hands and declare allegiance to him.

The convention made clear Trump would not ease his grip on the GOP.

Then came his October campaign rally blitz, with the president continuing to dismiss voters’ economic worries and issue cryptic threats about the accuracy of American elections and what he called Democrats’ collective intention to “cheat.”

By the end of his midterm rally sprint, Trump increasingly went for laugh lines, a striking contrast to his fire-and-brimstone events in 2016. Audiences of his supporters from Gastonia, N.C., to Corpus Christi, Texas, to Dalton, Ga., to Salina, Kan., to Wasilla, Alaska, chuckled along at times.

His closing-argument event Monday at the Reflecting Pool that sits between the Lincoln Memorial and the World War II memorial on the National Mall offered another example of Trump’s predilection for talking about matters relatively unimportant to voters. He talked about pool liners, his White House ballroom and other non-election issues.

By late Tuesday night, Trump appeared to have moved on, telling The New York Times he would accept the outcomes of the election results.

In other media interviews, the GOP president said he was ready to meet congressional Democrats at the negotiating table but that he had his veto pen ready.

“We’re going to work with the Democrats to make sure things go well for our country,” he said on a Politico livestream Tuesday. “They’ve already called and they want to work with me.”

Niels Lesniewski contributed to this report.

All the news about The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Ahead of the release of Rockstar Games’ Grand Theft Auto VI, Nintendo is launching its own fall juggernaut: a Switch 2 remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time.

The remake, which is releasing 28 years after the launch of the original version on Nintendo 64, has a much more realistic visual style and some modern improvements like a jump button, a way to sprint, full voice acting, and a “Threads of Time” feature that helps you keep track of your progress. But while the game has a new coat of paint, it appears many elements will still remain faithful to the classic N64 release. Nintendo has also launched a special version of the Switch 2 and Switch 2 Pro Controller with Triforce imagery to celebrate the game.

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time remake is scheduled to release on November 5th. You can follow along with all of our coverage of the game right here.

For the Ocarina of Time remake, Nintendo developers said in a new interview that they wanted to “preserve the experience of spamming the Roll Attack to forward roll your way across Hyrule Field.” I have very fond memories of rolling around the world in the original.

In the remake, you can still spam Roll Attack, but you can also dash.

The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time launches later this week, and in the lead-up Nintendo has put out the first half of a lengthy developer Q&A, covering everything from what changed gameplay-wise, to the new visual style, which was inspired by puppet shows. As art director Aya Shida explains:

Neither puppet shows nor practical effects films reproduce reality exactly as it is. Even so, both create a convincing sense that the characters truly exist within their respective fantasy worlds. We also felt that there was some overlap between these forms of expression and the distinctive nostalgia evoked by the original game.

The long-awaited remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time for Nintendo Switch 2 launches next Thursday, November 5th. In other words, you have a week remaining to secure your physical edition with a $10 discount at Amazon and Walmart. Once the game launches, the physical version will almost certainly go from $59.99 to $69.99 while the digital version will stay at $59.99.

Nintendo had the unenviable task of reimagining its Nintendo 64-era masterpiece for the Switch 2, and based on our hands-on time with some sections of the game, it’s a lot of fun to play. The graphics and art style are fantastic, and the controls have been modernized to be simpler to pick up for those who are already experienced with third-person action games, including other modern Zelda titles.

I’ve really warmed up to the game’s art style, especially in this new trailer. And after reading my colleague Cameron Faulkner’s hands-on preview, I’m looking forward to returning to Hyrule even more. The game launches on Switch 2 on November 5th.

With its upcoming remake for the Switch 2, Nintendo might have nailed the best thing about The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time: its timelessness. The 1998 3D action adventure game for the Nintendo 64 that pioneered so much — 3D graphics, movement, exploration, openness — has been reengineered with changes that make it feel more modern, and surprisingly terrifying at times. Based on my early hands-on time with the game, it’s shaping up to be the best-looking, best-playing version of the game that addresses the most frustrating aspects of playing the original.

More than just sanding off its roughest edges, Nintendo also fitted Ocarina of Time with modern trappings to make it more welcoming to those who were introduced to Zelda through more modern releases, like Breath of the Wild. The remake has a new progression marker, called Threads of Time, that’s viewable when you pause. It also has Zelda Notes integration within the Nintendo Switch app to let you view game maps and other info on your phone. Oh, it also has a dedicated jump button.

It was briefly shown during Nintendo’s The Legend of Zelda 40th Anniversary Direct today, but Hori’s website now has a listing for its $49.99 electronic ocarina. You can play your own songs by pushing buttons, play one of 12 pre-programmed songs from the games, use it with the Ocarina of Time remake’s sound recognition feature, or connect headphones for a private jam session.

Link’s next adventure now has a release date. During a Legend of Zelda stream for the franchise’s 40th anniversary, Nintendo announced that the upcoming Ocarina of Time remake for the Switch 2 is launching on November 5th. The release date makes Ocarina of Time one of just a few big-budget titles launching in the same month as Grand Theft Auto VI.

Nintendo first teased the remake in June, though it didn’t reveal much aside from a brief trailer. Today’s event provided a clearer glimpse at how the company is approaching remaking one of its most influential games and the first 3D Zelda adventure. The updated version of Ocarina of Time will be the first Zelda release that’s exclusive to the Switch 2, and longtime Zelda producer Eiji Aonuma described the remake as “a faithful interpretation using modern technologies.”

Nintendo announced a remake of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time during its Nintendo Direct presentation on Tuesday. It will launch sometime in 2026 on Nintendo Switch 2. More details will be announced “in the future.”

This new title is the first major release in the franchise since 2024’s Echoes of Wisdom, which starred Princess Zelda herself. Echoes followed 2023’s Tears of the Kingdom, a direct sequel to Breath of the Wild — both of which got upgraded Switch 2 versions alongside the launch of the new console last year. In November, Nintendo also launched the hack-and-slash title Hyrule Warriors: Age of Imprisonment.

Coinbase gets New Jersey staking relief after three years — Details

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Coinbase has secured a significant legal victory in New Jersey after state regulators formally dropped their lawsuit against the cryptocurrency exchange over its staking services.

According to Molly Abraham, VP of Legal at Coinbase, the state has also lifted the cease-and-desist order previously imposed on Coinbase, allowing the platform to resume offering staking services to customers in New Jersey.

The dispute dates back to 2023, when New Jersey’s Bureau of Securities took action against Coinbase. It alleged that its staking program constituted an unregistered securities offering.

Regulators argued that Coinbase had failed to register its staking-as-a-service program under state securities laws. As a result, the exchange faced restrictions that prevented eligible New Jersey residents from participating in its staking program and earning rewards on their digital assets.

With the lawsuit now dismissed and the enforcement order lifted, Coinbase can restore staking access for its New Jersey customers.

As expected, Coinbase’s Chief Policy Officer, Faryar Shirzad, welcomed the decision, describing it as an important development for staking access across the U.S.

However, the company also indicated that its regulatory battle is far from over. Wisconsin, Maryland, California, and Washington remain among the states where staking-related restrictions or disputes need to be addressed.

Coinbase has witnessed growing traction in tokenized stocks lately, with it ranked second among tokenized stock issuers by decentralized exchange (DEX) trading volume over the past seven days.

In fact, according to Token Terminal, its tokenized assets now account for 26.7% of the total trading volume, narrowly trailing Robinhood’s 27.3%. Source: Token Terminal

However, broader market indicators point towards weakening investor demand. For instance, the Coinbase Premium Index has remained negative for 39 consecutive days. This indicated that Bitcoin [BTC] has consistently traded at a discount on Coinbase compared to global cryptocurrency markets.

On 30th October, the index dropped to -0.2682%, reflecting heightened selling pressure and weaker buying interest following the Binance hack. In fact, at press time too, it remained negative at -0.1847%. This suggested that selling pressure had not completely subsided.

Meanwhile, $COIN stock has been struggling too. It was changing hands at $137.83 at press time after a drop of 3.98%.

Morgan Stanley recently downgraded COIN from Equalweight to Underweight and slashed its price target from $258 to $120. Analyst Michael Cyprys cited deteriorating confidence in the company’s earnings recovery, weaker trading activity, a softer revenue outlook, and uncertainty surrounding the crypto market.

The investment bank also reduced its 2027 revenue and adjusted EBITDA forecasts by 28% and 47%, respectively.

All of this further coincided with the exchange reporting net income of $719 million for Q3 2026, compared to $433 million during the same period last year.

This means that despite New Jersey’s victory and its growing presence in tokenized stocks, there are significant headwinds still surrounding Coinbase’s business outlook.

'It Follows' Star Officially Addresses Her Return to Another Iconic 2010s Thriller Franchise

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

In a new interview, Monroe addressed the possibility of returning for a sequel

Maika Monroe has built a career as a modern scream queen. After a breakout 2014, when she starred in both It Follows and The Guest, she became a key figure in horror, continuing to revisit the genre in everything from Longlegs to Victorian Psycho. This year, both It Follows and The Guest have been a part of the horror conversation: It Follows just wrapped a sequel, wonderfully titled They Follow, while The Guest just received a quasi-sequel with Onslaught, which director Adam Wingard set in the same continuity as his previous 2014 film.

Monroe notably doesn't appear in Onslaught (there are no repeat characters), but will she ever return to The Guest universe? In a recent interview for her upcoming film, Victorian Psycho, Monroe sounded hopeful.

“I love Adam so much,” she tells Polygon. “We grabbed coffee not too long ago, and he's awesome, so I would love to work with him again, so we'll see.”

The Guest remains one of Wingard’s most beloved films (and this is the guy who directed Godzilla vs. Kong). Written by Simon Barrett and starring Monroe and Dan Stevens, the story follows a charming visitor named David (Stevens) who arrives at the Peterson family home. There, he reveals that he is an army veteran and was a close friend to their son, who died while on tour in Afghanistan. David soon integrates himself into the grieving family, coinciding with strange and violent events taking place in the town. Anna (Monroe), the family’s eldest daughter, begins to grow suspicious of his behavior and past. The Guest marked one of Stevens first freaky roles, an edge that has since become a defining aspect of his career.

Onslaught came out earlier this year and marked a reunion between Wingard and Barrett, who’ve built a long partnership throughout their careers, collaborating on films like A Horrible Way to Die, You’re Next, and the V/H/S franchise.

The film stars Adria Arjona as an army vet with PTSD, who’s forced to defend her community and her daughter against three Terminator-like army experiments who’ve escaped a nearby facility.

Onslaught also features Stevens as Dr. Hans Kammler, an aging German scientist with Nazi ties and the mastermind behind the super-soldiers stalking the protagonist. Wingard confirmed that the events of Onslaught take place near the town that’s featured in The Guest. Both films are a part of his and Barrett’s "KPG-verse," named after the shadowy organization that runs the super-soldier experiments and appears in several of their projects.

Even though Stevens plays different characters in both films, Wingard suggested there might be a connection between them. “I think he’s related to the genesis of the David character in some form [or] fashion, and I think that’s something that maybe we’ll more literally explore hopefully in future installments,” he told The Wrap.

Stevens, for his part, is happy to continue to explore that universe. “We’re definitely open to more KPG-verse. I don’t know whether a Guest 2, per se, is on the cards anytime soon, but you never know,” he told The Hollywood Reporter. “We’ve teed everything up for an Onslaught sequel as well, so watch this space.”

Whether we get a sequel to David and Anna’s stories or something new set in the same universe, the door is open. Monroe, Wingard, and Stevens all seem to agree: The world of The Guest is one they’d be happy to return to.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

iPad mini OLED vs. iPad mini: What's new?

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Apple's new iPad mini gets a bunch of upgrades, but they come at a cost.

Apple doesn't update its iPad mini every year, which is why each new generation usually gets a huge performance boost. The new iPad mini is powered by the 5-core, 2nm A20 Pro SoC, which Apple says is up to 2.7 times faster in graphics performance and up to 4.5 times faster in AI workloads than the A17 Pro in its predecessor. It comes in the same three storage configurations as last time, and the 512GB model features an extra CPU core and a dual 16-core Neural Engine.

While both models support Apple Intelligence and Siri AI, the A20 Pro should be more suited to demanding on-device AI tasks. The outgoing iPad mini offered 8GB of RAM, and while Apple doesn't officially list how much memory its phones and tablets have, the new iPad mini likely has 12GB of RAM. Battery life claims haven't changed, but the new iPad mini charges faster (capable of hitting 50 percent in 30 minutes) with a compatible adapter.

Unfortunately, color options have been reduced to just Midnight and Starlight. The price has also increased: the new iPad mini costs $200 more, starting at $699 for the 128GB model. If spec'd with cellular connectivity and a nano-texture glass finish, the 512GB iPad mini will set you back a whopping $1,249 — that's iPad Pro money. We'll let you decide if it's worth it; for now, let's look at the changes Apple has made to its compact tablet offering this year.

The big iPad mini upgrade this time is the OLED panel. That makes it the only other iPad in Apple's lineup with an OLED display aside from the iPad Pro. It's roughly the same resolution as last time, but can get much brighter at up to 1,200 nits of peak HDR brightness. OLED also brings other benefits: the new iPad mini is the same height and nearly the same width, but is thinner at 5.4mm and lighter at 255 grams — likely thanks to the thinner OLED panel. In comparison, the A17 Pro iPad mini maxes out at 500 nits of SDR brightness, weighs 293 grams and is almost an entire millimeter thicker at 6.3mm.

The new iPad mini also features a slightly larger 8.5-inch display, owing to the bezels that are now 24 percent thinner. The only major letdown is the lack of ProMotion. Plenty of users on X have understandably expressed disappointment about Apple sticking with a 60Hz display on a $700 product in 2026. However, in an interview with Christopher Lawley, Apple's vice president of Hardware Engineering, Dave Pakula, said that the "display experience is about more than just a single spec" and that OLED's faster pixel response time "makes everything really smooth."

If you're coming from an older iPad mini, the display on the new one will be a huge upgrade, especially for creative workflows involving HDR content. Apple Pencil Pro support is still present, too, making the new iPad mini a compact, color-accurate canvas for artists and designers.

Despite its nearly identical design, the new iPad mini is Apple's first water-resistant iPad. One of the ways it has achieved an IP67 rating is by replacing the speaker grilles with its new Acoustic Display system. This involves tweeters placed between the glass and aluminum body, with woofers firing through the display. The result, apparently, is punchier bass, clearer treble and more volume. It's similar to the Huawei P30 Pro's acoustic display, though that particular setup was largely designed to replace the earpiece speaker for phone calls rather than deliver a full stereo speaker experience like the iPad mini.

Connectivity upgrades are also present, including Wi-Fi 7, Bluetooth 6 and Apple's new C2 cellular modem system, which it claims should deliver up to 50 percent faster cellular data speeds.  Authentication is still handled by the Touch ID-enabled power button, and while the iPad mini's optics seem largely unchanged, the front-facing camera is now positioned along the tablet's longer edge, making it better suited for video calls in landscape orientation.

The new iPad mini runs iPadOS 27 and supports Apple's most powerful on-device AI model with improved dictation and the option to adjust Siri's pace and expressiveness. While only the 512GB variant gets the extra CPU and Neural Engine cores, the 5-core A20 Pro SoC in the lower-end storage variants still delivers a substantial performance boost over the A17 Pro. With so many good AAA titles like Control and Death Stranding now available natively on the App Store, the new iPad mini is shaping up to be a portable powerhouse for gamers.
